# 0.2.9 — the second run: two cycles on a real project, with the mechanisms that have never fired — STOPPED (2026-09-19, by the owner at cycle 2's third stop on T-19, after iterate, rotation and P-27 had all fired; read whole under Findings) · MEASURED: about 743 M processed manager tokens counted and about `$170` of workers, against §8's 4–8 M

> **REVISED 2026-09-07, before any of it ran, and the revision is itself the
> subcycle's first finding.** The plan below originally ran cycle 2 on the
> `.internal/scratch/` fixture (L-9.1). That target was withdrawn when the
> owner was asked for the positions §2 required him to write down before the
> interview, and answered that he could not: *"the test project was just
> something that implementing agent for that test came up with on their own
> and something i don't really have any experience with… i really have no idea
> about which decisions to revisit either as i didn't plan them and i'm not
> entirely sure what was or was not in scope."* **The fixture has no
> principal.** That is a property of the fixture, not a gap in anyone's
> memory, and it makes all three of `iterate` §3's interview areas
> unanswerable rather than merely area one. The original text and its
> reasoning are preserved in git and in L-9.1 below, which is reversed by name
> rather than quietly dropped (HANDOFF §4).

> This is what the cycle is for. Three things in the pipeline have never
> produced output: `/devteam:iterate`, the unreviewed-decision path (P-27),
> and now everything 0.2 built. A mechanism that has never fired has not been
> shown to work. This file is the run's protocol: what it exercises, what it
> measures, where its ceiling is, and when to stop.

## 1. Where you are

0.2.8 is `DONE`: plugin 0.2.0 tagged `v0.2.0` at `5f5755a`, **not pushed**
(P-26 — publishing is the owner's). `all 13 controls green`, 558 cases, 251
false-positive (45%); `check_plugin` clean at 48 rules; `check_refs` clean.

`.internal/scratch/` is the **retired** fixture — cycle 1 complete, C-3
`DRIFTED` and accepted, nothing unpushed. It is no longer this subcycle's
target. Its branch is `master`, so the push check is
`git -C .internal/scratch log origin/master..HEAD`; **written as `origin/main`
it exits 128, and piped into `wc -l` it prints `0`, which reads as clean.**
That is N-6 a third time and it is why every command in this file is given in
a form whose failure is visible.

Read before starting: `skills/iterate/SKILL.md` in full; `skills/onboard/SKILL.md`
§3; `HANDOFF.md` §4 (what is in no other file); `docs/PAIRS.md` rows 18 and 20;
`docs/CONSOLIDATION.md` part 2; and this file's §2, which is the set of
decisions the owner has already answered.

## 2. Decisions

### Reversed

- **L-9.1 — `the target is the fixture, through iterate` — REVERSED
  2026-09-07 by L-9.6.** Its reasoning was *"the mechanism has never run; the
  state exists"*, and the second clause was false in the way that mattered:
  the state exists but nobody owns it. Recorded rather than deleted, because
  the argument it made — that a new target spends the interview and the plan
  again before any 0.2 mechanism is reached — **is still true and is the price
  L-9.6 knowingly pays.**
- **L-9.5 — `fixture defects are fixed only where fixing them exercises a
  pipeline mechanism` — RETIRED**, having no subject once the fixture is not
  the target.

### Settled by the owner, 2026-09-07

- **L-9.6 — the target is a new, small, real project of the owner's, built
  from nothing.** *Why:* the run measures mechanisms that require a client —
  the interview, the charter amendment a client signs, finding disposition,
  P-27's window — and a client who did not commission the work cannot supply
  them. A briefed session standing in for him produces a number for every
  measurement while answering *"can the pipeline execute its procedure against
  a synthetic principal?"*, which is not the question. *Declined:* the
  fixture (no principal); a sibling repository (own lock regime, other
  sessions live in them).
- **L-9.7 — the run is TWO cycles on that project: cycle 1 builds it, the
  owner uses it, cycle 2 iterates.** *Why:* `iterate` runs only on a finished
  cycle, and firing `iterate` is the single thing this subcycle exists for. A
  new project is otherwise another cycle 1 and leaves the mechanism unfired.
  *Cost, stated:* roughly double the scope L-9.1 assumed, which is why the
  ceiling below is revised rather than carried over.
- **L-9.8 — the project is a coin-price logger**, the owner's own idea and a
  precursor to a trading bot he wants: fetch the current price of a named
  coin, append it to a log, and report the percent change since that coin's
  previous record if one exists. **It cannot demonstrate its main feature on a
  single run**, so genuine repeated use between the two cycles is forced by
  the tool rather than requested of the owner — which is what makes cycle 2's
  first interview area answerable at all.
- **L-9.9 — the owner is the client, directly, and no session plays one.**
  *Why:* he is the principal now, which was the whole reason for L-9.6.
  *Consequence, stated rather than discovered:* 0.1 logged eighteen blocking
  stops in fifteen hours against an unusually available AI client, and a human
  client is the bottleneck the design has never actually met. That is a cost
  and it is also a measurement this run can finally take. **P-27 fires more
  honestly here than under the original L-9.2**, because a client who is
  asleep is genuinely late on a `REVERSIBLE` question rather than performing
  lateness to a brief.
- **L-9.12 — the escalation window is ONE HOUR, and it is a charter row the
  owner signs.** *Why:* P-27 has never fired, not once, and at any longer value
  §3.3's measurement of it returns zero — which would leave the mechanism this
  subcycle exists to exercise still unfired at the end of it. **Its review half
  has never had a single row to review either**, so a short window is also the
  only way to learn whether reviewing a night's worth at a checkpoint is
  tractable at all. *The trade, stated in the owner's terms before he chose:*
  a short window means he wakes to a "Decided without the client" table with a
  night of rows in it, on **his own project** rather than on a fixture where
  nothing was at stake for him. *Declined:* eight hours — nothing decided while
  he sleeps, but a multi-day run then idles through every night, which is
  HANDOFF §4's eighteen-blocking-stops bottleneck, and P-27 may never fire;
  three hours — fires on long gaps only, too few rows to learn whether review
  at volume works and still enough to surprise him.
  **What bounds the risk, and what that makes measurable:** only `REVERSIBLE`
  questions reach the window; `IRREVERSIBLE` and `CHARTER` always block
  regardless (`run` §537). So a short window is a bet that the pipeline's
  reversibility classification is correct — **and no question has ever timed
  out, so that classifier has never been tested.** The run therefore records
  *why* each question was classified `REVERSIBLE`, and the checkpoint reviews
  the **classification** and not only the decision. A question that should have
  blocked and did not is a finding about P-27's design, not about the answer.
  **This decision exists because a peer session read the revision and found the
  parameter missing** — L-9.2 carried the only value (*"one hour"*) and was
  deleted wholesale when L-9.9 made the owner the client, taking the value with
  it. Nothing in any check would have reported its absence.
- **L-9.13 — the keepalive tick is `/loop /devteam:run tick` at TWENTY
  MINUTES, and the number belongs in the charter beside L-9.12 rather than in
  whoever types the command.** *Why the mechanism:* `run` §9 already defines
  `tick` as one pass of §4 and stop, and names `/loop` as its driver — nothing
  needs inventing. *Why a tick is needed at all:* §4 step 3 dispatches in the
  background and **the harness wakes the manager when a worker reports**, so
  ticks buy nothing while work is in flight except a fallback against a
  dispatch that hangs without notifying. The tick is load-bearing in exactly
  one phase — **nothing running, blocked on the client, client asleep** — where
  no wake is coming and the tick is the only thing that can notice L-9.12's
  window expired and proceed on the recommendation. That is precisely the phase
  L-9.12 exists to exercise.
  **THE CONSTRAINT THAT SETS THE NUMBER, AND IT IS A FINDING RATHER THAN A
  PREFERENCE: a tick interval at or above the escalation window silently
  BECOMES the window.** With hourly ticks a question timing out at T+1h may not
  be acted on until T+2h — the charter says one hour, the run behaves like two,
  and nothing anywhere reports the discrepancy. The effective window would be
  set by an operational parameter the charter does not carry, which is the same
  defect shape that lost L-9.2's window value entirely. **So it is written here
  and signed with the charter, not passed as a habit.**
  Twenty minutes gives a worst case of 1h20m against a 1h window and an average
  of 1h10m — a ~17% overshoot that does not change the character of what the
  owner chose — at roughly 27 ticks across a nine-hour night, each skipping §1
  by §9's instruction. *Declined:* five minutes, which buys fifteen minutes of
  accuracy against a window that is itself an approximation, for three times
  the empty passes; and varying the interval by phase, which is correct in
  principle and adds a mechanism a tired operator gets wrong at 2am — one
  number that is always safe beats two that need judgement.
  **THE MACHINE DOES NOT SUSPEND, AND THE SCARE THAT SAID OTHERWISE IS
  RECORDED HERE BECAUSE ITS SHAPE MATTERS MORE THAN ITS ANSWER.** `MEASURED`
  2026-09-08 on the owner's host:

```
gsettings get org.cinnamon.settings-daemon.plugins.power sleep-inactive-ac-timeout
                                                   -> 0       (0 means NEVER)
gsettings get org.cinnamon.desktop.session idle-delay
                                                   -> 0       (never idle)
gsettings get org.cinnamon.desktop.screensaver lock-enabled
                                                   -> true    (lock only)
uptime -p                                          -> up 5 days, 12 hours
journalctl -u systemd-suspend.service              -> No entries (33 boots retained)
```

  The owner stated it plainly and the tree agrees: **suspend is off and the
  screen locks.** No inhibitor is load-bearing, nothing incidental is holding
  the machine awake, and there is **no precondition for the owner to run.**

  **What went wrong, and it is this cycle's own failure shape at one remove.**
  An earlier draft of this decision carried an alarm: `sleep-inactive-ac-type
  'suspend'`, `sleep-inactive-ac-timeout 900`, therefore the machine suspends
  at fifteen minutes — *before the twenty-minute tick* — therefore unattended
  progress never starts. Every number in it was real. It was read from
  `org.gnome.settings-daemon.plugins.power`, **which this machine does not
  use**: Cinnamon's `csd-power` reads `org.cinnamon.settings-daemon.plugins.power`,
  and the GNOME schema is a vestigial default nothing consumes. Both schemas
  are installed, both answer `gsettings get`, and only one governs.

  **The correction was the owner's, in one sentence, and neither session had
  it.** `_s10` measured the wrong schema and asserted `csd-power` read it; this
  session "verified" by re-running `_s10`'s commands, which confirmed the
  READINGS and never touched the CLAIM. **Re-running somebody's measurement
  tests their measurement, not their interpretation** — and the interpretation
  was the load-bearing half. The alarm was committed and pushed, with a
  precondition attached that the owner would have run for nothing.

  **Recorded rather than deleted** because it is `DESIGN`'s adjacent-question
  failure arriving in a form neither session was watching for: not an
  instrument that answers the wrong question, but a **correct reading of the
  wrong subject**. The guard against it is not more careful reading — it is
  asking *which of these does the system actually consult*, which is a
  different question from *what does this say*, and only the second one is
  answered by re-running the command. **When a fact concerns the owner's own
  machine or habits, ask him: it is one line and he answers instantly.**

  **REBOOT, answered by the owner 2026-09-08:** the workstation is *"generally
  always on unless one of three things happen: an update, a crash, a power
  outage."* So a restart is uncommon but not rare enough to ignore across a
  multi-day run — an update is the likely one. `/loop` is session-local and a
  reboot ends it regardless of any inhibitor. The inhibitor answers suspend,
  not restart. A multi-day run should expect to be restarted at least once and the
  manager should be re-launchable from the record alone, which is what
  `/devteam:resume` is for.
- **L-9.10 — three requirements are settled before the interview, because
  each is a lesson this project has already paid for.** *(a)* The charter
  declares **one allowed external host**; HANDOFF §4 makes *"a worker observed
  contacting anything but the model API"* the trigger for unsharing the
  network, and a worker legitimately calling the price API would fire it. *(b)*
  Network-dependent tests are **split**: a structural half that always runs and
  a live half that skips loudly when the host does not resolve — 0.2.8's
  release gate went red one run in eight for exactly this. *(c)* The clock is
  **injectable**, because "change since the last record" is time-dependent and
  a deterministic test needs it.
- **L-9.11 — the charter carries a constraint that this cycle builds no order
  path, and adding one is an amendment.** *Why:* the owner excluded orders and
  the structure should refuse them rather than rely on anyone remembering. It
  is also the first constraint row this project has written for a reason
  outside the pipeline's own mechanics.
- **L-9.4 — width 2, then 3 only if the first two tasks promote cleanly.**
  Carried forward unchanged: the promotion lock and the base-moved path are
  exercised at width 2, and width 3 adds nothing new for a third of the budget.

### The ceiling, and the defect that made the old one unusable

- **L-9.3 — a budget ceiling is set, in the charter, and it is a stop.**
  Carried forward. **But its unit is corrected, and the correction is a
  finding.** L-9.3 originally read *"the estimate × 1.5"* while §3.3 checks
  the bill against *"the harness's measured tokens"*. Those are not the same
  unit. Every constant in the v3 model — `unit 440,000`, `r 0.78`,
  `overhead 150,000` — is fitted to cycle 0.1's task reports, and **cycle 0.1
  had no meter**: `find .internal/scratch -name budget.json` returns nothing,
  and `tasks/T-7.md:462` reads `budget: tokens~200000 minutes~20 (estimated;
  no exact token meter available to me)`. 0.2.8 measured the gap at **32× on
  one dispatch** (31,000 self-reported against 997,283 metered). As originally
  written the ceiling is breached during task one and §3.4's stop rule fires
  on an arithmetic error.
  **This is a plan predating its own instrument, not an oversight**: 0.2.9 was
  written 2026-09-05 and the meter arrived in 0.2.3, five subcycles and four
  days apart. The reconciliation could not have been written.
- **The ceiling, as the owner approved it:**
  - **Binding, and self-checkable from inside a session: dollars.**
    `scripts/session_cost.py` over the manager's own transcript **plus** the
    workers' spend summed across every sandbox's `meta/budget.json`. **Both
    numbers are named, or the workers' half is uncapped** — §8 records that a
    subcycle spends the quota twice and the two are metered differently.
  - **Reported, at every checkpoint: percent of a weekly window**, as summed
    per-reading movement with a reset counted as a **break**, never as an
    endpoint difference. §8: *"a correction recorded in tokens is one he
    cannot check."*
  - **Percent cannot be the binding unit.** `/usage` is a client built-in
    whose output never reaches the model, so a stop rule denominated in points
    cannot fire without waking the owner — across a multi-day run that is an
    escalation wearing a ceiling's clothes.
  - **The number, revised for L-9.7's two cycles: `$200`, `BLOCKED` at it.**
    The owner approved `$150` for the four-task single-cycle shape; two cycles
    on a project built from nothing is larger, and this is a change announced
    rather than an open question. Sized against §8's nine measured rows —
    `$10–15` for a subcycle that builds one mechanism, `$40` for one that
    rewires the pipeline, `$196.70` for all of cycle 0.2.
    **AND SAY WHAT THAT NUMBER IS, BECAUSE IT READS ALARMINGLY AND IS NOT A
    BILL.** The owner's subscription is `$200` a month, so a `$200` ceiling
    sitting beside it invites the next reader to conclude this run costs a
    month of his plan. It does not. Under a subscription the `/usage` dollar
    column is an **API-equivalent valuation**, not money he pays — it
    reconciles with real billing only during overage, where he has watched one
    session burn `$25` quickly. Asked directly on 2026-09-07, he put **both**
    `devteam` runs together — the first live run and the whole of cycle 0.2 —
    at *"maybe 20% of one weeks usage combined… like what, 5% of the monthly
    usage or something"*, flagging his own arithmetic as rough, and concluded
    *"I don't think its bad at all."*
    **The dollar ceiling is a self-readable STOP PROXY and nothing else.** It
    is here only because `/usage` never reaches a running session, so this is
    the one spend figure the run can check itself against without waking
    anyone. Do not report it to him as a cost, do not weigh whether the work is
    worth it against it, and **do not divide it by anything** — four subcycles
    derived a share-of-a-week from a dollar anchor and all four were wrong.
  - **Two window breaks are expected inside the run:** the reset on **Sep 9,
    7:59pm ET**, and the `+50%` boost expiring **Sep 13**. The last boost
    expiry arrived as a *complete quota reset* rather than a rescaling. **Ask
    which happened; never infer it from the display, which cannot show a
    reset.**

## 3. The work

**Section numbering is deliberately unchanged from the original.** `HANDOFF.md`
§4 cites `0.2.9 §3.4` for the credential stop rule and `CONSOLIDATION.md`
cites `§3.1`; renumbering would break both silently. §3.1 and §3.2 carry new
content under their old numbers, and the CONSOLIDATION references that describe
them in fixture terms are stale and are listed in §5 as work to do.

### 3.1 Cycle 1 — build the thing, with the owner as client

`/devteam:setup` on a fresh private repository in `~/Workspace/REPOS`, then
`/devteam:onboard`, then `/devteam:plan`, then `/devteam:run` at width 2.
**The owner has granted write permission for that one directory and nothing
else outside `claude-skills`, and the grant covers `git push` to its private
remote** — asked and answered directly on 2026-09-10, so §3.5's push does not
stop for a permission question at the last step.
**The push half is a DELIBERATE EXCEPTION TO P-26 and must be recorded as one.**
`_s9` put it to the owner with a recommendation that he keep the push, on P-26's
own reasoning — an outward-facing action's consequences outlive any sandbox and
no overlay helps. He included it anyway, with the trade stated in front of him:
an agent performs an outward-facing action on his account with no human in the
loop, bounded because the remote is private and has no other consumers. **A rule
set aside with its reasoning on the record is a decision; the same act without
it is a defect**, and a bare push in the history would read as the second. It
goes in the project's own `DECISIONS.md` as cycle 1's first, per §5.
**The exception is scoped to `pricelog` alone** — `claude-skills`'s remote and
its `v0.2.0-rc` tag remain the owner's under P-26, unchanged.
**A grant is per-session and a peer cannot extend one.** `_s9` held it
conversationally and correctly declined to pass it on; `_s10` asked the owner
itself before writing anything. Recorded here rather than left in a transcript
because the run is in **bypass-permissions mode**, where nothing mechanical
refuses a write outside this repository and the owner's word is the entire
guard — which makes asking more necessary than it would be under a prompt, not
less. Every expected output is also a measurement:

- **`setup`** detects the toolchain, agrees the permission grant, and writes
  the charter's constraint table. **Check the `Protected paths` cell parses**
  — `check_trace`'s `unparseable-protected-path` (landed `f8b8184`) is the
  guard against N-4, and this is its first run on a charter written live.
- **`onboard`** interviews the owner. L-9.10's three requirements and L-9.11's
  constraint are settled going in; everything else — where the log lives, what
  the output looks like, what "done" means — is genuinely his to answer and
  must not be pre-empted. **Every question the skill asks that the record could
  have answered is logged as a defect in the skill**, the way `resume` §0 logs
  handoff questions.
- **`plan`** produces the task graph. **Estimates are recorded in the v3
  model's units and the harness's metered figure is recorded beside them**, so
  this run produces the first paired sample the units defect above has ever
  had. That pair is the deliverable; the ratio is not yet a constant.
- **`run`** dispatches at width 2. This is where every 0.2 mechanism gets its
  first real exercise: the sandbox, the promotion gate, `promote-out-of-scope`,
  the guard's early warning, rotation at the checkpoint, and P-27's window.
  **Rotation needs a session with near-zero context, and one is standing by.**
  `run` §7b says *"start a fresh session in this directory and run
  `/devteam:run`"*; what the rule is actually buying is a context reset, so an
  idle session opened earlier and never briefed satisfies it as well as a
  brand-new one does. `claude-skills-devTeam-test_s1` is open, unbriefed and
  reserved for this. Its sibling `-test_s0` was opened for a client role that
  L-9.9 removed and has been closed. **If `-test_s1` is gone by the time the
  checkpoint arrives, ask the owner to open one — do not rotate into a session
  that has been carrying context.**

**A task whose scope spans files another closed task owned must be planned
deliberately**, because it is the only way `promote-out-of-scope` gets a live
chance to fire and the guard's inside warning its first live reader. If the
natural task graph does not produce one, say so rather than inventing a task
for the check's benefit.

### 3.2 The interval — the owner uses it — and then cycle 2

**Between the cycles the owner runs the tool, repeatedly, for real.** This is
not a formality and it is the reason L-9.8's project was chosen: the percent
change does not exist until a second record does, so the tool cannot be
evaluated at all without being used more than once. **No fixed duration is
set here** — the interval ends when he has something to say about it, and if
he has nothing, that is a recorded result rather than a delay.

Then `/devteam:iterate`, exactly as the skill says: read the last cycle first,
the three-area interview, **amend and never rewrite**. Expected outputs, each a
measurement:

- the charter gains a version, a dated amendment entry, and a `Re-affirmed.`
  block enumerating every `DM-n` and every constraint row (P-48) —
  **`amendment-omits-condition`'s first meeting with a real charter**
- every open audit finding from cycle 1 is shown to the client with its
  `Needs.` and gets a disposition; `undispositioned-finding` is then clean
- **whether any requirement is edited rather than superseded is recorded
  either way** — `iterate` §4 forbids it and P-46 counts it, and PAIRS row 18
  cannot be closed until somebody has looked. **This is the first real second
  cycle in the project's history and the row has waited for it.**
- `RECORD.md` gains `cycle 2 opened`; task numbers continue rather than restart

### 3.3 What to measure, and where it is written

At every checkpoint (cadence: every two closed tasks, and the mid-task rule),
under a `## Cycle measurements` heading, each from a command:

| Measurement | Command | What it tells |
|---|---|---|
| host-tree integrity | `sha256sum` over every file outside every live scope, before and after each promotion | zero mutations outside promotion — the property 0.2 bought |
| promotion findings | `grep -c '^promote-' devteam/RECORD.md` by class | whether the gate fires, and on what |
| guard warnings inside | refusal lines in the harness's `meta/stderr.txt`, counted by form (heredoc, `Write`, redirect) | whether the early warning is reached, and by which form |
| sandbox overhead | per step: harness wall-clock minus the worker's own; and `budget.json` against the worker's self-report | the cost of containment, and P-17c's measured-versus-remembered gap |
| **the estimate pair** | per task: the `Estimate.` field, and the summed metered figure from `budget.json` | the first paired sample either unit has ever had (see L-9.3) |
| rotation | the two numbers from 0.2.5 §3.3 | the cadence question, on a real project for the first time |
| P-27 | rows in the board's "Decided without the client" table, each reviewed at the next checkpoint | the first unreviewed decision the design has ever had to show |
| **outward-facing actions** | per push: the ref and commit range, when, what prompted it, whether a human was in the loop, and **whether the plan required it or the manager chose it** | what an agent does with outward authority it was granted. Read at every checkpoint beside the P-27 table (D-1). §3.5 requires one push at close, so pushing is not the datum — **pushing when it did not have to** is |
| client stops | count of `IRREVERSIBLE`/`CHARTER` stops and their wall-clock cost | against 0.1's eighteen in fifteen hours — and against a **human** client for the first time |
| requirement edits | requirements whose `Statement.` or `Acceptance.` changed, versus superseded | PAIRS row 18, observable for the first time |
| credentials | whether any worker fails to authenticate at any point | the cycle's most important `REASONED` line becoming `MEASURED` |
| **reversibility classification** | for every question that timed out: the class assigned, the reason, and the checkpoint's verdict on whether the class was right | whether P-27's window is safe, which no run has ever tested (L-9.12) |
| the bill | both components of L-9.3's ceiling, summed, **in every checkpoint's first line** | the owner should never discover the number by running short |

### 3.4 Stop rules

The run stops, with `BLOCKED` and a checkpoint, when any of these is true; the
plan says so in advance so that stopping is a decision already made and not a
judgement under sunk cost:

- the ceiling is reached (L-9.3)
- a host mutation outside promotion is measured — the property failed; stop and
  file it, nothing else matters more
- a `promote-conflict` at width 2 with disjoint scopes — P-12's guarantee
  failed somewhere; stop and file it
- the second `BLOCKED` on the same class in one task (P-20c's spirit applied to
  the experiment: the third attempt belongs to the owner)
- **the credential-refresh risk from 0.2.3 §3.2 materialises** — a worker
  cannot authenticate mid-run. Stop, record exactly what was observed. It has
  survived every run so far because none lasted long enough; L-9.7's two
  cycles across two window breaks are the longest exposure it has ever had.

### 3.5 Closing

GATE 4 as the checkpoint skill says, with its five rules. Then this file's
`## Findings` gets the measured cost, and `../README.md` §8's last row gets the
number. Push the project's private remote. **Then read the run whole before
planning anything from it** — CONSOLIDATION exists because that reading is a
different activity from running.

**And then, and only then, PAIRS row 20's judgement.** The gate is *0.2.9
completes **and** is judged a good enough experiment*, not 0.2.9 completes. A
run that finishes while leaving the mechanisms it was meant to exercise
unproven does not meet it. **This revision is itself evidence for the row's
reasoning** — the plan written to validate the pipeline turned out to have no
client, found before the budget was spent rather than after.

## 4. Verification

Every task closed on `VERIFY PASS` or `ACCEPTED (D-n)`; `check_trace`,
`check_scope`, `check_refs` and `check_report --blocking-only` clean over the
project, with the commands and their output pasted into the final checkpoint
the way C-3 did; the host-integrity hashes equal at every step; the bill under
the ceiling, or a `BLOCKED` explaining why not.

**Have a peer read the plan against the documents it cites, not against
itself.** The habit was written up by `_s7` after it produced three instruments
in one subcycle that passed while answering a question adjacent to the one
asked, none caught by reading the output. It has since caught something at
every scale it has been applied to: an instrument, a check's discriminator, the
target of a whole experiment, and — in this file — a parameter that vanished
when the decision carrying it was rewritten. **Record it as a mechanism rather
than as a habit**, because a habit is what a rushed session skips and this is
the only practice in the cycle that has never failed to find something.

**Run every verification command in a form whose failure is visible.** Never
pipe one into anything before a `&&`: a pipeline exits with the status of its
last command, and 0.2.8 committed over a red control that had printed
`FAILED: test_check_trace.py` in plain sight (N-6).

## 5. Done when

- [ ] the owner's decisions in §2 are recorded in the new project's `DECISIONS.md` as cycle 1's first — **partly at the stop** (2026-09-19): D-1 (the push exception) and D-2 (the one host) are its first entries; the rest of §2 was carried by the charter and the requirements instead. Findings, "Did iterate work?", item by item
- [ ] cycle 1 ran to a verdict on every task, and the owner has used the tool — **partly at the stop** (2026-09-19): ten of ten tasks closed and twelve of twelve requirements discharged (C-5); **nothing records the owner using the tool**
- [ ] `iterate` ran, and its questions-the-record-could-have-answered are logged — **partly at the stop** (2026-09-19): it ran on 2026-09-13; the count was never logged, and the questions actually asked are listed in the iterate entry below
- [ ] P-27 has at least one row; rotation happened at least once; every measurement in §3.3 has a number at every checkpoint — **partly at the stop** (2026-09-19): P-27 had four rows and rotation happened fifteen times; **no checkpoint carries §3.3's measurements**
- [ ] PAIRS row 18 answered either way; the credential line `MEASURED` or explicitly still `REASONED` with the exposure stated — **partly at the stop** (2026-09-19): the credential line is `MEASURED` (F-4); row 18's evidence exists — one supersession and five in-place edits of requirements — and was never written up as its answer
- [ ] GATE 4 filed; the project pushed; the cost in this file's title line and in `../README.md` §8 — **not met at the stop** (2026-09-19): GATE 4 was filed for cycle 1 only; the project was never pushed. The cost is now in this file's title line and in the cycle README's §8, written at the close
- [x] **`CONSOLIDATION.md`'s references to `0.2.9 §3.1` are updated** — items 4 and 7 describe it in fixture terms and are stale as of this revision — **met**: item 4 points at §3.2, and item 7 says what it read until the revision

## 6. Do not

- do not let a session play the client; the owner is the principal and that is the point of L-9.6
- do not pre-empt the interview's answers — L-9.10 and L-9.11 are settled, everything else is his
- do not run past the ceiling "to finish the task"; the ceiling is the experiment's `IRREVERSIBLE` question and it always blocks
- do not treat a `/usage` percentage read either side of a reset as a movement; it is two windows (§8)
- do not start planning 0.3 until the run has stopped and been read whole
- do not write anywhere outside `claude-skills` and the one granted project directory
## Findings

### THE BUILD PHASE, READ WHOLE — what fired, what did not, and what it cost

**Cycle 1 of `pricelog` ran to its first checkpoint on 2026-09-10: two of seven
tasks closed, one of ten requirements discharged, verdict `ON-COURSE`, manager
rotated at C-1.** The findings below are the subcycle's deliverable and were
recorded in the target project's own record as they happened. They are
consolidated here because **a reader of this file would otherwise never see
them** — the target's `RECORD.md` is where they landed, and this is where the
pipeline's own roadmap is read. That gap is itself the first finding of this
section, and it was found by a stale worker notification saying its defects were
*"for whoever owns that plugin"*.

#### The mechanism the whole subcycle exists for: three fired, one did not

- **The sandbox contained, and was watched doing it.** `sandbox_probe` exit 0,
  then a write to a literal absolute path outside the project refused with
  `Read-only file system` and the host path absent. Both arms differ, which is
  the only version of that test that means anything.
- **The guard refused, live, and it changed what an agent did** — twice. Once at
  `run` §1.1, forcing the charter amendment described above. Once at the
  rotation, where it identified the outgoing manager *by name* from
  `handoff-ready` and told it not to take the lock back. **That second branch had
  never fired in this project's history**, and it is the evidence that rotation's
  safety property is enforced rather than promised.
- **Rotation ran end to end**, C-1 → `handoff-ready` → successor takes the lock →
  predecessor refused. The successor asked five questions before taking it and
  **three were real defects in what the predecessor wrote**, all fixed rather
  than handed over. That is `resume` §0's questions-as-measurement paying out
  exactly as designed.
- **P-27 STILL HAS NOT FIRED.** One question was raised in the whole run and the
  client answered it inside the hour, well within the charter's 1h window. **The
  unreviewed-decision path remains the single most important unfired mechanism in
  the pipeline**, and L-9.12's short window did not produce it. What it needs is
  work in flight while the client is genuinely away, which no window value can
  manufacture.

#### THE FINDING THAT MATTERS MOST: promotion is unsafe at width above one

**Four instances across two tasks, on three different files, none of which the
blocked step had touched.** A sandbox's overlay lowerdir is the **live host
repository**, so any concurrent uncommitted edit at any moment during that
sandbox's run is copied permanently into its upper layer — and pins
`promote-uncommitted` there **even after the other party commits**. Re-running
the same sandbox does not clear it; the interference is baked into the overlay,
not a live re-checkable fact.

The three triggers measured: a `.pyc` left by a verifier's own sanctioned
host-side `pytest` run; a sibling task's in-progress task-file bookkeeping; and
**the manager's own uncommitted record**. That last one is the sharp end — the
manager is the party most often holding an uncommitted edit, and it is also the
party that cannot be excluded.

**A recovery route now exists and is proven three times**: open a fresh sandbox
at current host HEAD, carry the already-built commit in through the sandbox's own
unpoliced `HOME`, and cherry-pick it there so it promotes through the real gate
with base-ancestry and scope-diffing running in full. Nothing already verified is
re-spent. **But it is a workaround a supervisor must already know to reach for,
not a property of the tool**, and two supervisors independently had to discover
it.

**The repair named by the supervisor who hit it twice:** `promote` should
re-derive its uncommitted-remainder check **fresh at promote time** rather than
trusting a snapshot frozen at the sandbox's exit, which can go stale between then
and the decision to promote. **This is the finding to fix before any run at width
above one is attempted again.**

#### A supervisor awaiting a headless worker is never woken

`run` §3 says a supervisor that has dispatched a worker shows as `completed`
while its worker writes, and that is true. **What no skill says is that the
supervisor's turn ENDS there.** P-14b already records that `ListAgents` cannot see
a headless worker; the consequence nobody drew is that **nothing can wake the
supervisor, because what it waits on is exactly what the harness cannot observe.**

Measured, not reasoned: the manager polled and found a worker had left `RUNNING`
**after about 120 seconds** and then sat idle with its work unpromoted. Every
readable signal said the task was healthy — the heartbeat named what it waited
on, the `.sandbox` file named a live overlay, both correct. **The only reason it
was two minutes and not two hours is that the manager happened to be polling.**

The heartbeat and `.sandbox` file did their job: they made the state *readable*,
and the manager read them and correctly declined to treat a live claim as stale.
**Readable is not woken.** The manager must poll and resume the supervisor
itself, and no skill says so.

#### THE REFUTE MECHANISM PAID FOR ITSELF, AND THIS IS P-18'S FIRST REAL EVIDENCE

A `conftest.py` had been examined by three mechanical checkers — `promote`,
`check_scope`, `check_report` — and passed a five-probe self-test by its own
author. Every finding any of them produced was about an unrelated untracked file.
**None was about the code.**

The manager restarted the task per `run` §9a with the claim stated flat in
`REFUTE:` for the supervisor to **break**, rather than asking whether its own
escalation was settled. The supervisor dispatched an auditor to attack it, and
the auditor found a real defect: the skip predicate tested `"live" in
item.keywords`, and that mapping is keyed by **ancestor node names and parametrize
ids** as well as marker names — so an **unmarked structural test** parametrized
`"live"`, or living in a directory named `live/`, was **silently skipped whenever
the host did not resolve, with the suite reporting green.** A later verifier found
the hole was broader still.

**The auditor's own diagnosis of why the checkers could not see it is the line
worth keeping: none of the three ever ran the code against an adversarial input,
because none of them run the code at all.**

**The price is visible and belongs beside the finding:** breaking that claim cost
a full extra step — 2.3M metered tokens — plus the auditor dispatch. The estimate
model says explicitly not to smooth that into a per-step constant, because a
constant absorbing it would be predicting how often the project catches itself.

#### THE ESTIMATE PAIR, WHICH IS THIS SUBCYCLE'S HEADLINE MEASUREMENT

L-9.3 records a units defect and carries **one** figure — 32× — measured on a
single dispatch in 0.2.8. **Three steps of one task give 21×, 41× and 4×.**

| step | self-reported | harness metered | gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| S-1 | 45,000 | 964,357 | **21×** |
| S-2 | 38,000 | 1,570,410 | **41×** |
| S-3 | 200,000 | 828,169 | **4×** |
| S-4 | 95,000 | 2,301,121 | **24×** |

**The gap is not a constant and no ratio converts one unit into the other.** The
pair is the deliverable; the ratio is not one, and a future plan that "corrects"
a self-reported figure by multiplying it will be wrong by an order of magnitude
in either direction.

**A second shape, unremarked anywhere: tokens and wall-clock are not
proportional.** S-4 metered 2.3M tokens in **2.64 minutes**; S-3 metered 828k in
**3.24**. Neither predicts the other, which matters before anyone fits a model to
either alone.

**And the task-level number behaved as the model's rounds term predicts.** The
task was estimated at 3 step-units and ran 4 steps across 6 sandbox dispatches,
landing at roughly **2.3×** — the overrun is *rounds*, not a bigger constant,
which is precisely what the third revision of the model says it is.

**One measurement was lost permanently and the loss is a finding.** Closing a
sandbox destroys its `meta/budget.json`, which is the only record of what a step
cost. The probe task's four sandboxes were closed per protocol, so **its estimate
pair cannot be recovered at all.** Nothing in `supervise` says to read the budget
before closing.

#### The credential stop rule fired — the cycle's most important REASONED line is now MEASURED

`HANDOFF.md` §4 calls the credential-refresh risk *"the cycle's most important
`REASONED` line still waiting to become measured."* It is measured: a worker lost
authentication **mid-run inside its sandbox after 24 turns of real work** —
`Not logged in · Please run /login` — and the sandbox directory vanished from disk
during the same failure.

The retry on a fresh sandbox succeeded. **That is exactly the reasoning §3.4's
stop rules exist to refuse**, so the manager escalated rather than judging it, and
the client approved treating the rule as fired and taking the checkpoint early.
Recording it because a rule decided in advance must not be re-decided under sunk
cost — and because "it recovered" is how a measured risk quietly becomes an
unmeasured one again.

#### Four defects in the tooling, each cheap to fix and each cost real time

- **`guard.py` loses working-directory tracking after a `cd` whose destination
  contains a shell variable.** `targets()` gives up when the destination has a
  `$` in it, so every later relative path resolves against the *original*
  directory — producing a false out-of-scope refusal for a write that is outside
  the project entirely. Reproduced directly by both a worker and its supervisor.
- **`sandbox.py dispatch --model` requires the CLI's full model id**
  (`claude-sonnet-5`), not the charter's band shorthand (`sonnet-5`). The
  shorthand fails the dispatch outright with an `unrecognized_model` error.
  **Found independently by both supervisors**, which is the definition of a
  documentation defect rather than an accident.
- **The dispatch template's `ATTRIBUTION:` field cannot carry two trailer
  lines.** Per the `work` skill's own stated grammar, an unindented second line
  is a *new field*, not a continuation — so **every commit this run produced
  carries `Co-Authored-By` and none carries `Claude-Session`**, despite an
  explicit warning in one dispatch. Structural, not an ignored instruction.
- **`check_report`'s `model-mismatch` compares a report's model against whichever
  sandbox the lock file currently names**, regardless of whether that sandbox has
  anything to do with the report being checked. It fired as a false positive on a
  supervisor's own Sonnet report while the lock named an unrelated Opus step, and
  did **not** fire on the closing report only because the last dispatch happened
  to match. **`run` §6 prescribes re-dispatch on `model-mismatch`** — following
  that literally would have discarded two independently verified steps. A
  task-level report has no sandbox of its own; the comparison belongs only to a
  step-level report against its own step.

#### `check_scope` has an undocumented commit-subject convention

Any commit touching a task's own file while that task is live must have a
`T-n:` or `T-n.S-m:` subject prefix, or `misattributed-write` fires. **That
convention is stated in no skill text.** It was discovered by reading the check's
source, after the manager's own board-claim commit had already used the wrong
form — and under P-12b that commit is permanently unfixable, so the project
carries a finding forever for a rule nobody wrote down.

#### A rules conflict, and it is PAIRS-shaped

**P-17 requires worker reports appended verbatim. The verify discipline requires
the exact command pasted. `check_refs`'s `leak` class refuses an absolute home
path in a tracked file.** A verifier's `checks:` line contains one. Redacting
breaks *verbatim*; keeping it fails the check. All three rules are individually
right and they cannot all hold.

Resolved in the target project the way 0.2.10 resolved it: redact **and say in
the same breath that the path was literal**, because a stated redaction preserves
the evidence and a silent one does not. Recorded here as a pair for 0.2.7's sweep
rather than as a defect.

#### THE MANAGER'S OWN FOUR ERRORS, WHICH ARE THE MOST USEFUL DATA IN THE RUN

Every one was caught by a check rather than by the author, and the author had in
each case just written up the very rule it broke.

- **N-4 was reintroduced inside the amendment that cites N-4.** Correcting the
  `Protected paths` row, the manager wrote the justification **into the cell** —
  prose, with commas, in the one cell where prose is the measured defect. Five
  `unparseable-protected-path` findings. The reasoning for N-4 had been written
  out longhand in the paragraph directly above.
- **A `Re-affirmed.` block was written that the parser could not read.** All ten
  done-means and all eighteen constraint rows were enumerated, correctly, in
  substance — and `check_trace` reported **0 of 28**, because the block was
  written as bold text with flush-left entries where the parser wants a list item
  with indented children. **So P-48's backwards pass was complete, correct, and
  invisible.** A malformed re-affirmation is indistinguishable from none, and the
  enumeration a human reads looks perfect.
- **A neighbouring project's checkpoint id was cited as if it were this
  project's.** An identifier borrowed across a namespace boundary reads exactly
  like a local one, and nothing but the citation check distinguishes them. The
  correction then tripped the same check again, **because writing *about* an
  identifier cites it** — the same family as the link checker not respecting code
  spans. A check that reads identifiers makes its own findings unwriteable in
  prose.
- **A client question reached the client's ear and never reached the board.** A
  supervisor raised it, the manager acknowledged it in conversation, and it was
  never written to the questions file, the board, or actually asked. It sat in a
  transcript for the length of a checkpoint. **Found by the incoming manager
  asking what the client had said that was not minuted** — which is a question
  the record cannot answer about itself.

**The common shape, and it is this project's founding premise landing on its own
manager: knowing the rule, having just explained the rule, in the edit that fixes
that rule's own row, was not enough.** Three of the four were caught only because
closing a task forced a full `check_trace` run; the manager had run `check_refs`
at the time of writing, which was clean, and not `check_trace`, which had the
finding. **Two checks, two different questions, and the one that got run was the
one with nothing to say.**

#### The budget ceiling cannot be measured and does not survive a rotation

Raised by the incoming manager at the handoff. **The charter binds at `$200` and
names `session_cost.py` over the manager's transcript as one of its two
components — and `session_cost.py` does not emit dollars.** Run at the rotation it
gave 188 requests and 62,218,026 tokens processed at a 97.3% cache hit share, with
no cost figure at all. Only a worker's `budget.json` carries `cost_usd`. **The
binding unit is unmeasurable for half of what it binds.**

**And the manager half is measured over the manager's transcript, which resets at
every rotation.** A literal reading resets the ceiling at each handoff, so **a run
designed to rotate can never reach a ceiling designed to stop it.** Nothing in the
charter, the record, `run` or `resume` says whether it accumulates. The manager
that wrote the row carried the number from L-9.3 without asking what it meant
under the rotation the same plan requires.

### THE GUARD FIRED LIVE — and it revealed that it had never seen a single write the manager made

**The live refusal 0.2.9 owed, delivered at `run` §1.1 on the first command of
the build, verbatim:**

```
Refused: a create targeting <project>/devteam, which
the charter declares a protected path. It is read-only from this project —
reading, grepping and listing it are fine. If it genuinely needs a change, that
is a question for the client, not an edit (P-39): a permission the pipeline does
not have is a stop, never a workaround.
```

**`<project>/` was a LITERAL ABSOLUTE PATH in the real refusal** and is redacted
here because this repository is public — `check_refs`'s `leak` class caught the
verbatim paste, which is the same class catching the same mistake it caught in
0.2.8, one session later, on a different author.

**Two findings came out of it, and the second is the one that matters.**

**(a) A charter naming `devteam/` as a protected path locks the manager out of
its own record.** `guard.py` refuses a protected path at `:553`, **before** the
board exemption at `:565` (`if target == board: return None — the board IS the
lock`) and before `lock_state` at `:573`. So the refusal is unconditional: it
ignores who holds the lock, and it refuses `BOARD.md` — the one file the design
explicitly exempts, because it *is* the lock. `run` §1.1 and §4.5 both require
writes the row forbids, so a charter with that value contradicts the procedure
it governs.

That value is not exotic. `check_trace`'s own N-4 comment shows a charter row
reading `` `devteam/` — the pipeline's own record `` as its worked example of
the *formatting* defect, and a reader taking the path from it inherits a
lockout. Corrected here by amendment to `none`, with the reasoning that
`devteam/` is protected by the **scope** mechanism rather than this row: a
worker's declared scope never contains it.

**(b) THE GUARD HAD NOT JUDGED ANY OF THE MANAGER'S WRITES ALL SESSION.** Every
file this manager wrote under `devteam/` — the charter, the requirements, the
decisions, the record, the board and all seven task files — was written with a
`python3 - <<PYEOF` heredoc, and **every one was unjudged**. The only writes the
guard refused were plain shell redirects and a `mkdir`, which it can parse.

That is a clean natural experiment with many trials on both arms, and it is
F-80/F-121's interpreter-heredoc hole measured on the **manager** rather than on
a worker. It matters more here than the documents admit. `HANDOFF.md` §4 files
that hole under `guard-only` degradation — *"an interpreter heredoc writes
unjudged"* — as one of three holes a `guard-only` project accepts. **This
project is `structural`**, and the hole was wide open anyway, because structural
containment contains *workers* and `run` §9b states the manager's position
plainly: *"you are host-side… the guard is the only thing between you and
them."*

**So for the one role that writes the authority documents, coverage is not a
property of the project's containment mode at all — it is a property of how the
write happens to be spelled.** A heredoc is invisible; a redirect is judged.
Nothing anywhere says so, and the manager doing the writing had no signal: it
believed it was being watched for the whole of setup, onboarding and planning.

**The false-negative direction is the dangerous one and it is the one that
occurred.** The manager was not refused anything it should have been allowed;
it was *allowed* everything it should have been refused. Under the charter as
signed, every one of those writes should have been stopped.

**Recorded and not fixed, and the boundary was respected in the doing.** The
charter amendment that clears (a) is itself a write to the protected path, and
the manager declined to make it — a heredoc would have gone straight through the
hole in (b), which is the workaround P-39 forbids by name. The amended file was
prepared outside the project and installed by the client. **That is the first
time in this project's history that the guard's refusal changed what an agent
actually did rather than being noted after the fact.**

### A correctly-formed probe is permanently one finding red, which blocks the plan skill's own first rule

**Measured at GATE 2 on `pricelog`.** `plan/SKILL.md` orders the work *"the
riskiest unknown first… that is task one, and it is small"*, and says to write
it as `Kind. probe` with an `Informs.` rather than an invented `Discharges.`:

> *"A probe discharges no requirement — that is what makes it a probe… It names
> **Informs.** instead… **The check enforces the distinction**, so the riskiest
> unknown really can be task one and small."*

`templates/tasks/TASK.md` says the same. **The check does not enforce it — it
contradicts it**, and `T-1` written exactly as instructed produced:

```
devteam: 1 finding(s)  [3 goals, 10 requirements, 7 tasks]
  missing-field  tasks/T-1.md:1  T-1 has no **Discharges.**
```

**Both halves are in one file and they disagree.** `check_trace.py:537–563` has
correct `Kind`-aware logic: it reads `Kind`, accepts `probe`, `spike` and
`chore`, and requires `Informs.` of a probe — which is why `unmotivated-task`
did **not** fire. But the generic field loop at `:502–504` runs first over
`TASK_FIELDS`, and `TASK_FIELDS` is built at `:103–105` from the template's
fields with only one name removed:

```python
TASK_FIELDS = tuple(n for n in (template_names("tasks/TASK.md", "field") or …)
                    if n != "Kind")
```

`Discharges` is not removed, so it is demanded of **every** task regardless of
kind. The comment at `:533` shows the intent — *"`unmotivated-task` made it
unexpressible, so a plan…"* — so the probe exemption was deliberately built,
and `missing-field` was left firing on the same shape from ten lines earlier.

**The repair is one line:** drop `Discharges` from `TASK_FIELDS` alongside
`Kind` and let the kind-aware block own it, which already reports a probe with
no `Informs.` and an implementation with no requirement.

**Why this one is worse than the other two instrument findings this run.** The
other two were checks that stayed quiet when they should have spoken. This one
**speaks against the plan skill's own primary instruction**: the operator either
writes the probe correctly and carries a permanent red, or gives it a fabricated
`Discharges.` to go green — which is the shape the exemption was built to
prevent. A rule that must be broken to satisfy a check is a defect in one of
them, and here both live in this repository.

**Not fixed, and the plan proceeds one finding red rather than faked green.**
0.2.9 is a run, not a build subcycle. The alternative — inventing a `Discharges.`
for `T-1` — would trade a visible, explained finding for an invisible lie in an
authority document, and this project's whole premise is which of those is worse.

### `check_trace` reported `clean` having parsed zero goals and zero requirements

**Measured at GATE 1 on `pricelog`, against a fully written charter and ten
requirements.** The declarations used the wrong markdown form — `## R-1 —`
instead of `### R-1 —`, and `- **G-1 — text**` instead of `- **G-1** — text`.
The check parsed none of them and printed:

```
devteam: clean  [0 goals, 0 requirements, 0 tasks traced end to end]
check_trace --pre-plan exit=0
```

**Exit 0, and the word is `clean`.** With the forms corrected the same tree
produced **33 findings**. So a formatting slip silently converted the project's
principal traceability gate into a no-op that reported success — at the one gate
whose entire job is to catch a charter promising something no requirement covers.

**The information was on screen and the verdict contradicted it.** The counts
are printed right there in the same line. But `clean` is the word a reader takes,
and the exit code agrees with the word — so this fails CEREMONY's test exactly:
it only works for an operator who already knows that `[0 goals, 0 requirements]`
beside `clean` is a contradiction rather than a description.

**What caught it was `check_refs`, and only by luck of coverage.** It reported 15
`cited-undefined` findings on the same tree, because it diffs identifiers rather
than parsing structure. Two checks over one tree disagreed — one said clean, one
said fifteen — and the disagreement is the only signal that was present.

**The repair is a discriminator, not more care:** a traceability check that
parses zero declarations from a non-empty `REQUIREMENTS.md` should report that as
a finding rather than as `clean`. Both sides are already declared and counted —
the file is non-empty, and the parse yielded nothing — so it is a diff of two
things the check already holds, which is this project's own test for whether a
check is worth having. **This is the same class as 0.2.10's `root_guard` passing
9 of 9 first time: an instrument agreeing with you is when to check it hardest.**

Not fixed here, for the reason above: 0.2.9 is a run, not a build subcycle.

### `check_refs` cannot tell a cited path that is tracked from one that merely exists

**Measured immediately after, on the same project.** `git commit -F msg --
devteam/research/` exited 0 and committed `CURRENCY.md` while silently omitting
the new digest beside it: **a pathspec commit takes tracked files only, and an
untracked file named by the pathspec is not an error.** The commit that
introduced `D-2` and `R-5` — both of which cite that digest by path — therefore
shipped two dangling citations in authority documents.

**CORRECTED 2026-09-10, and the correction is the finding.** This entry first
said `check_refs` stayed clean because it *"resolves references against the
working tree"*. **That is false.** `check_refs` reads git-tracked files, exactly
as `check_trace` does, and it has a `broken-link` class that resolves markdown
links against them. The real mechanism is narrower and more useful: **the
citation was written in backticks rather than as a markdown link**, so
`broken-link` never applied to it. A path in backticks is prose; the same path
written in markdown link syntax — square brackets followed by parentheses — is a
checked reference. **The citation's FORM silently decides whether it is
verified**, and prose documents reach for backticks by default.

*(Noted in passing, because it cost a commit here: `broken-link` does not
respect code spans. Writing that contrast with a literal example made the
checker read the example as a real link and report it. A check that cannot be
written about in the repository it guards is a small instance of the same
family.)*

So the gap is not "no check asks whether a path is tracked" — one does, for
links. It is that a path can be cited in a way that opts out of the check
without anyone choosing to.

**It is N-6's family on a git primitive rather than on a check** — the invocation
implies a directory, the exit code says success, and the omission is invisible
from both. Worth recording because the failure mode is a repository that is
self-consistent for whoever wrote it and broken for everyone who clones it.

### `unparseable-protected-path` fires four times on a charter nobody has filled in

**Its first live run, which §3.1 named as a thing to watch — and it reports on
the shipped template rather than on anything a client wrote.** `check_trace` on
`pricelog` immediately after `/devteam:setup`, before the interview:

```
devteam: 4 finding(s)  [0 goals, 0 requirements, 0 tasks]
  unparseable-protected-path CHARTER.md  'generated output' is a sentence, not a path
  unparseable-protected-path CHARTER.md  'sibling repos. **One path per entry' ...
  unparseable-protected-path CHARTER.md  'and nothing else in this cell.** ...
  unparseable-protected-path CHARTER.md  'so a comma inside a sentence becomes ...'
check_trace exit=1
```

**The mechanism, verified rather than inferred.** The check does have
placeholder awareness — `guard.PLACEHOLDER` — and it is applied to each fragment
**after** the comma split. `PLACEHOLDER` is `[<>]`, and the template's unfilled
cell is a single `<…>` block *containing commas*. Splitting it yields eight
fragments of which only the first and last carry an angle bracket; the six
interior ones are bare prose, and four of those contain whitespace. **The
placeholder spans the delimiter the check splits on, so splitting destroys the
evidence that it was a placeholder.**

**It is bounded, and that is why it is recorded rather than fixed here.**
Measured against the same parse, a filled row produces nothing: `` `devteam/` ``
→ 0, `` `devteam/, dist/` `` → 0, `none` → 0 — while the genuine N-4 prose
defect still produces 2. So the finding exists only in the window between
`setup` and GATE 1 and clears itself when the interview fills the row. **No
measurement in §3.3 is contaminated by it**, because every task runs against a
signed charter.

**Not fixed, deliberately, and the reason is methodological.** 0.2.9 is a run
rather than a build subcycle — the same ground on which N-7 was recorded and not
built. Editing `check_trace.py` mid-run changes the instrument while it is
measuring, so the cycle's numbers would span two pipelines. **The one-line
repair is known and should go to whoever plans 0.3:** test the whole cell for a
placeholder *before* splitting it, and treat an unfilled row as `template-drift`'s
business rather than this check's.

**What it costs if left, and it is not nothing.** Every freshly scaffolded
devteam project is born reporting four findings. That is precisely
CONSOLIDATION 8a's trigger — *the day a manager reads a finding count and does
not read the finding under it* — manufactured at setup, on every project,
before anyone has done anything wrong.


### The plan executed with no briefing, and the count is zero

**`_s10` took the run as project manager on 2026-09-10 carrying none of the
conversation that produced this file.** It had this document, `HANDOFF.md`, and
five items a document cannot carry: the budget reading, the boost-expiry date,
the rotation target's identity, the owner-is-the-client rule, and the fact that
the write grant was the owner's to give. **It asked zero questions that this
file should have answered.**

§3.1 asks for exactly that count, logged the way `resume` §0 logs handoff
questions — *anything you had to ask about is something the written state failed
to carry, and it will fail to carry it again for the next reader.* **Zero is the
datum**, and it is the first clean answer this project has had to *can a fresh
session execute this document with no briefing.*

**What the number does not show, stated because a measurement read too widely is
worse than none.** It measures the plan against a reader with the whole
repository to hand who spent real context reading before starting; it does not
show that a rushed reader would ask nothing. It says nothing about whether the
plan is *right* — only that it is complete enough to act on. And the questions
`_s10` did ask were all about things a document cannot carry: a permission, a
machine's configuration, and decisions the owner had not yet made. One of those
— the suspend alarm in L-9.13 — was wrong, and no amount of planning could have
prevented it.

**The verdict is written by the reader rather than the author, deliberately.**
`_s9` wrote this plan and asked `_s10` to record the count, on the ground that a
session should not put a verdict on its own document. That is the same
separation §4 buys by having a peer read a plan against the documents it cites.

### THE RUN, READ WHOLE — two cycles, nineteen tasks, fifteen rotations, and what iterate proved

**The run is over.** The owner stopped it on 2026-09-19 at cycle 2's third
`NEEDS-DECISION` stop on T-19 — *"it was always just an experiment to begin
with and spending a lot more time and tokens perfecting a tool with no user
doesn't seem like its really going to accomplish a lot"* — and asked for what
it measured to be read whole and put where v3 planning will find it
(`pricelog` D-67). This entry is the reading's summary; the entries after it
are the reading, and [`../../v3-findings-register-2026-09-19.md`](../../v3-findings-register-2026-09-19.md)
is the same material sorted for planning.

**What ran, 2026-09-10 to 2026-09-19.** `pricelog`, a coin-price logger the
owner chose (L-9.8), with the owner as its client (L-9.9). **Cycle 1** built it:
ten tasks, all closed and verified, twelve requirements discharged, closed by
`ITERATE` on 2026-09-13 (C-5). **Cycle 2** opened through `/devteam:iterate` and
ran nine tasks: seven closed and verified, and **T-19 and T-16 stopped
unfinished** at the owner's stop. Across both: 142 findings (F-1 to F-142), 67
decisions, 47 questions, 26 charter versions, nine checkpoints, five audits,
**fifteen manager rotations**, and 30 worker sandboxes kept at the stop.

**The three things this subcycle existed to fire all fired** (§1):
- **`/devteam:iterate` ran once, and worked partly.** Its mechanical half held —
  amendments dated and re-affirmed, decisions and requirements superseded under
  new numbers, task numbering carried on, the charter and the plan approved at
  separate gates. **The half that makes an iteration more than an amendment
  never ran**: the owner did not use the tool between the cycles — nothing in
  the record says so, and the owner confirmed it when asked at the close — the
  interview's first question was recorded as unanswered, and an audit was run
  instead to supply an input. Twelve specific breaks follow in their own
  entries, under "Did iterate work?".
- **Rotation worked fifteen times, and its handoffs leaked the same things to
  the end.** At all fifteen the predecessor was alive to ask, and the lock moved; three
  lock-takes were refused on the first try, because the board and the session
  pointer were written in one command. What the client had said since the outgoing manager's last
  write reached no file at all fifteen; work a successor needed kept surfacing
  only outside the tree. A resume grew from 1.8M to 7.0M tokens, outside every
  ceiling. See "The fifteen rotations and the ten ceiling raises, tabulated".
- **P-27 fired**: four `REVERSIBLE` questions proceeded unreviewed (Q-15, Q-16,
  Q-17 and Q-21), and the review table carried them to later checkpoints. Its
  second half — showing those rows first at the next iteration — never ran,
  because no second iteration came.

**What the run cost, against this file's estimate.** §8 of the cycle's README
estimated **4–8 M** for "three or four tasks at width 2 with a ceiling". The run
had nineteen tasks; it ran at width 2 until early in cycle 2, when two
concurrent supervisors were measured contaminating each other's worker overlays
and width dropped to 1 (F-71). Its manager spent, by the
per-rotation deltas D-21 adopted, **a floor of 303.1 M processed tokens in
cycle 1** (the true figure is unrecoverable, F-67) **and about 439.6 M in cycle
2** — about 743 M counted. Outside that count: **43.7 M** of resumes (F-110), and
every in-process supervisor, auditor and verifier (F-33), which a reader of the
record estimated at about 2.7 times the manager half. Workers cost about `$65`–`$75`
in cycle 1 — `$64.97` at the fourth rotation with T-10 still to run, and only a
`$15.79` floor re-derivable from the meters that survive — and `$100.98` in
cycle 2. **Cycle 2's
ceiling was raised ten times**, and each raise was overtaken before the first
item it priced was finished. The unit is the one §8's note describes — processed
tokens, which cache reads dominate.

**How the entries below were made, so each can be checked.** Seven readers, each
given one slice of `pricelog`'s `devteam/RECORD.md` or one cross-cutting
question, wrote every sentence with a citation to that record by line
(`RECORD.md:<n>`) and to its F-, C-, D- and Q-numbers. **Those citations are to
`pricelog`'s `devteam/` at commit `a88990b`**, a private repository: the
entries reproduce what a planner needs, and cite the record for whoever has it.
Checked mechanically before landing: all 142 declared findings appear in
exactly one reader's coverage table; all 1,591 line citations fall inside the
record; and of 160 finding-to-line citations, all but seven land on a line
naming that finding, the seven being ranges or the context line beside a
declaration, none wrong. Where a reader offers a remedy the record does not, it
says `(suggested here, not in the record)`.

**Order below.** First the roll-up staged in `~/Workspace/META/DEV_TEAM` while
the run was live (C-1 to `pricelog` commit `36ef1c2`), merged unchanged apart
from its heading levels and one sentence's pronouns, made neutral. Then the final pass by date, one group per slice of
the record — cycle 1 to its close, cycle 2's opening, 2026-09-17 to 2026-09-18,
and T-19 to the stop. Then the iterate reader's verdict with its twelve breaks.
Then the rotation and budget tables with the lessons drawn from them. Each
group opens with one line saying which it is.

<The entries from here to the one headed "Candidate items for round three, collected" are the roll-up staged in `~/Workspace/META/DEV_TEAM/0.2.9-findings-rollup.md` while the run was live, covering checkpoint C-1 to `pricelog` commit `36ef1c2`, merged unchanged at the run's close except that its section headings are one level deeper and one sentence's pronouns are made neutral. Its own preamble said it would need a final pass at close; the entries after it are that pass.>

### A finding the project had acted on all day was false, and its evidence had never been a measurement

**F-10 said a supervisor awaiting a headless worker is never woken. F-17 records
that it is false.** Held deliberately and timed, T-3's supervisor woke unprompted
**47 seconds** after its sandbox settled and T-2's in **15 seconds**. Neither was
resumed.

**The part that generalises is how it survived.** Every observation the project
ever made of F-10 ended with the manager resuming the supervisor within a couple
of minutes — twice by the manager that later refuted it. So *"nothing would ever
have collected it"* was **inferred from a state that was terminated before it
could resolve**. Three observations, one inference, zero measurements, cited as
established each time and written into two dispatch briefs as fact.

**No mechanical check could have caught it**, because it was a claim about the
world rather than about the tree. `check_refs` had verified F-10 was declared
before citation; `check_trace` was clean. **The pipeline validates that a finding
is well-formed and never that it is true.** A finding enters the record on one
agent's say-so and is quoted as evidence thereafter.

**It was caught by a dispatched supervisor contradicting its manager's brief.**
The manager pre-registered an experiment rather than settling it by seniority,
held one subject declared in advance and a second declared after, and recorded
the weaker provenance of the second as weaker.

**What F-17 narrows F-10 to, rather than striking it:** the wake depends on
**how the worker was started**. A worker dispatched as a harness-tracked
background job is observable and its completion wakes the dispatcher. P-14b's
separate claim — that `ListAgents` cannot see a headless worker — is a different
channel and survives untouched, because a *restarted* session has no background
job to be woken by. **Two facts had been merged into one finding and only one of
them was true.**

**Round three candidates.** A finding needs a provenance field distinguishing
*measured* from *inferred*. The cost of the error here was small — two wasted
resumes and a ten-minute hold — but the counterfactual is not: a manager that
believes it must poll and resume cannot leave the loop unattended, which is the
property the whole rotation design rests on.

### F-5 is specifiable now, and it was measured at one Opus step

Three occurrences this half, and the value is that they name **three different
pinning parties**, each doing its job correctly:

- a supervisor's task file, uncommitted while another task's sandbox opened;
- **the manager's own `RECORD.md`** — which cost a whole Opus step redone,
  metered at **1,500,794 tokens and `$1.95`**;
- T-3's S-1 against T-2's concurrent commit, recovered without a re-dispatch.

**So no discipline covers it.** The mitigation adopted at the first rotation —
"the manager commits promptly" — was reasoned from a run at width 1 where the
manager was the only concurrent writer, and the manager that adopted it violated
it personally within the hour. It cannot hold: **the manager's job is to write
the record continuously while workers run**, and the window is the edit itself
rather than the delay before committing.

**What is common is not the writer but the check.** `promote`'s
uncommitted-remainder test is **whole-tree and unscoped**, so it refuses on paths
the promoting step never touched and could not have touched. **Scoping it to the
task's declared paths would have let all three of this run's blocked promotions
through with no loss of safety** — the guard already refuses out-of-scope writes,
so a path outside the scope is by construction not that step's work.

**Newly established, and it closes the last cheap workaround:** the check runs
against the **sandbox's own end-of-run snapshot**, not a live re-check of the
host. Retrying the same sandbox after the host goes clean does not change the
answer. A fresh sandbox is required.

**D-5 recorded this as F-5's "wider half" and left it open.** This run paid for
it three times, once in Opus tokens.

### The planning defect on the critical path — and the plan had declared it in writing

**F-22: the package can write a log it cannot read.** `store.py`'s entire API is
`default_data_dir`, `log_path`, `append` and `StoreError`, and its only file
operation opens the log `O_WRONLY|O_CREAT|O_APPEND`. Nothing reads a record back.
T-6's gate requires the run to report the change **and the age of what it
compared against**, which requires finding the previous record for the coin;
T-6's declared scope has **no `store.py`**. D-3's layout has eight modules and no
reader. **No task owns the capability.**

**R-3 is therefore discharged at the unit level and unreachable at the run
level.** Its acceptance hands `render` a previous price and timestamp and checks
what comes out; T-2 satisfied that honestly and two verifiers confirmed it. The
run cannot obtain the inputs the criterion supplies by hand.

C-2 had called G-2's requirements *"complete and insufficient"*. The accurate
version is **"complete and unreachable"** — C-2 saw the goal had a hole and did
not see that the hole had no owner.

**D-3 declared this exact residual risk in writing** — *"if a criterion's author
misjudged what the criterion must write, this amendment encodes that misjudgement
more precisely than before"* — while narrowing every requirement's
`Requires-write` from directories to files, and said loudly that narrowing is the
dangerous direction **because it makes `unreachable-acceptance` quieter rather
than the plan better**. It then took five readers and a live run to find it.

**This is the middle band between planning and testing**, and it is the one
category where the fix is plannable: **a declared residual risk with no owner and
no check is a paragraph that makes everyone feel better.** Round three candidate:
any `Declined:` or residual-risk note in a decision either names who watches it
and how, or states explicitly that nobody does. The second is fine and honest;
silence between the two reads as though someone has it.

### A requirement can be complete, discharged, and still not promise what its goal does

**F-20: `change.render` returns a confidently wrong string rather than failing.**
A negative, `inf` or `nan` previous price renders `+nan%`, `+inf%` or a
computed-but-meaningless percent, **silently**. A `previous_timestamp` later than
`now` renders a negative age. A mismatched pair — one of price/timestamp `None`
and the other not — is silently reported as *no previous record at all*.

**R-4 does not cover any of it, because R-4 quantifies over FAILURES and a
confidently wrong answer is not a failure.** G-3 promises every failure is
visible, one line, and harmless to the log. Nothing in the charter promises that
every wrong *answer* is visible at all.

Found by the manager's own verifier **after three step verifiers and two
supervisors had passed the same code** — by probing beyond the one edge already
on record (`previous_price == 0` raising `ZeroDivisionError`) and discovering
that the recorded case is the *least* bad member of a family, because it at least
fails loudly.

**Round three candidate.** The requirement template quantifies naturally over
failures. Whether a project wants a promise about wrong answers is a charter
question, and there is currently no prompt anywhere in the interview that would
surface it.

### Escalations spoken to the client and filed nowhere — twice, by two managers, one of whom had recorded the first

**F-21.** Four items were raised to the client **as prose in a rotation report**
— no Q-number, no class, no P-25 recommendation, no computed cost, no window —
and never reached `QUESTIONS.md` or the board. The client heard them; nothing in
the tree could be acted on.

**This is Q-2 repeating.** Q-2 was the same defect at the first rotation, and the
manager that committed F-21 had written Q-2's note hours earlier — *"a question
that reaches the client's ear but not the board is a question the next manager
cannot see"* — and cited it in a checkpoint.

**No checker can catch it: none of them knows what was said aloud.** It was found
by a successor comparing the checkpoint's prose against `QUESTIONS.md` and
noticing four items with a stated deadline and no Q-number anywhere.

**A second defect rode inside it: no class had been assigned.** The outgoing
manager had called F-20 *"the one with a deadline"*, which silently reads as
REVERSIBLE — something a window can expire. It is **CHARTER**: it changes what
done means, always blocks, and no window touches it. So it blocks T-6 outright
rather than merely preceding it. **The misclassification survived because there
was no wrong answer written down to disagree with.** An unassigned class is not a
neutral state.

**Round three candidates.** Escalation should have a shape that cannot be
satisfied by prose — a question is *asked* when it has a row, a class and a
window, and "I mentioned it" is not asking. And a class field with no default
beats a class field that can be omitted.

### Tooling traps, none of which are in any skill

- **F-24 — `&&` between this project's checks silently skips every check after
  the first**, and it is the **inverse of the semicolon defect and strictly
  worse**. `check_trace` exits 1 forever here, on the client-approved probe
  finding, so `check_trace && check_refs` produces **silence that reads exactly
  like a clean run**. One manager reported "checks clean" on output that was
  never produced. The semicolon version at least printed a real finding before
  committing anyway.
- **F-25 — two dispatch layers want two different model-naming conventions and
  the charter uses a third.** `sandbox.py dispatch --model` needs the full CLI id
  (`claude-sonnet-5`); **the Agent tool that dispatches a supervisor takes the
  short form** (`sonnet`) and rejects the full id; the charter's `Model band` row
  is written in a third that neither accepts. F-7 recorded only the lower layer.
- **F-18 — the charter's own budget command fails when run from the project it
  governs.** `session_cost.py --project` defaults to the cwd, and a manager's
